## The pit's excavator, container, cabin, drums, tyres and signs rebuilt (#556)

- **Why.** Jamie rode the pit (#472) and found its things basic and broken: an excavator in pieces that did not meet, flickering coplanar faces, a container with odd doors, a "hut on stilts" that was the plant's control cabin as a box, overlapping drums and tyres, and signs whose backs were unreadable. Each now has its own module (`excavatorModel.js`, `containerModel.js`, `siteCabinModel.js`, `quarryDebris.js`, textures in `quarryTextures.js`, the merging `Parts` kit in `parts.js`), built in a local frame and placed by `quarryProps.js`, which settles each on the ground's lie (`settle`: the middle at the average of four heights, tipped by no more than 0.25 rad) and puts its colliders in the world (`placeBoxes`).
- **Excavator.** Tracks of 42 shoes with grousers a side, sprocket, idler and rollers; a carbody and slew ring; a curved counterweight; a hood with exhaust, louvres and a tank; a cab on the left with pillars, glass, a door with frame, handle and hinges, seat and levers; a two-piece boom on ears and a pin, a tapering stick, a bucket with side plates, curved belly, teeth and linkage, and three sets of rams with hoses. Every joint is a pin at a shared point, and the stick's length is solved so the bucket's edge rests on the ground (`tests/quarryModels.test.js` holds each pin to the two parts it joins).
- **Container.** Real corrugation (`corrugatedGeometry`, 4 cm deep, 22 cm pitch) with dents pushed in by `dentGeometry`, corner posts and castings, two leaves on pins (one ajar) with locking bars, cams, handles, keepers, a hasp and padlock, on three sleepers. The leaves are built on their hinge and merged in turned.
- **Cabin.** A 3.6 by 2.4 m portable cabin that replaces the plant's stilted box, set beside the plant on whichever side is clear floor, its door and steps towards the plant: ribbed cladding cut round its openings (`cutWall`), a door ajar, two barred windows (one with its shutter swung back), gutter and downpipe, an air-con box, a desk and chair inside, and a bucket, chair, planks and pallet outside. It only exists where the plant does (the plant is not found a place on every farm's pit, as before).
- **Drums, tyres and signs.** Drums (lathe with hoops, rolled chimes, lid and bungs) and tyres (grooves and lugs), instanced by colour. `scatterClear` deals places so none overlap, a drum and a tyre are rolled off 5 to 9 m away, and the pile gets colliders. A sign's board is held off its post by straps, with the lettering on the front and plain galvanised steel on the back.
- **Flicker.** `coplanarOverlaps` (textured) is run over each model in a test; fixing it meant giving each overlapping bar a face a few millimetres from its neighbour's, never the same plane.
- **Cost.** About 22,000 more triangles (3.27M to 3.31M at spawn, 3.85M to 3.88M in the yard) and about 20 more draw calls at the spots that see the pit (632 to 656 at spawn, 2,704 to 2,727 in the yard; hilltop's 1,711 unchanged), with load time unchanged (2.9 s). Each model is merged per material, a sign's board and straps are one mesh in one texture, glass and decals cast no shadow, and drums and tyres are instanced. `smoke` hilltop 1,689 to 1,720 over runs, under its 1,730.

## The screening plant stands on a pad (#557)

- **Why.** Jamie rode the pit (#472): the plant was joined onto nothing and its conveyor went over the road and nowhere. It is now a small aggregate plant as a real one is laid out, a line on a flat pad: `screeningPlantModel.js` (built in the plant's frame, +x from hopper to heap, y = 0 the pad's top, merged per material, about 10 draw calls). Feed hopper (four plates, a rim, a grizzly, ribs, a ladder) on a braced frame over a jaw crusher with two flywheels and a motor, a first conveyor (side channels, belt and return run, drums, troughing idlers every metre, trestles on footings) up to a screen box on springs, a chute and a second conveyor on trestles that ends over the heap, a generator on a skid cabled to the crusher, and the excavator and site cabin placed beside it.
- **Dealt with the pit.** The floor is about 44 by 26 m and crowded, so finding the plant a place after the heaps failed on most farms. `dealPlant` (`plantSite.js`, pure, called by `dealPit` before the other heaps) picks the plant's own heap and lays pad, conveyor and room for the excavator in a line away from it, on floor off the pond, the climb's foot and both haul roads (`descentPath`, now exported, gives the roads' lower halves). The other heaps and the boulders keep clear (`inPlantsWay`). 6% of 200 test farms have no room and no plant; 15% have no room for the excavator beside it, which then stays where it stood.
- **The ground is level under the pad** (`plantPadWeight`, eased back in over 2.5 m): the floor's roughness and the foot of a wall had left it up to 1.5 m proud at one edge. Pad 12.4 by 6.8 m, its top 6 cm over the ground; a collider for it and the hopper, the screen's posts, each trestle leg and the generator, so you can ride up its lip and not through the hopper.
- **The conveyor ends over the heap**: head 0.9 m over its apex, the climb 19 to 25 degrees by design (up to 31 on a real floor, the test's limit is 33), trestle legs ending on the ground where each stands (some on the heap's flank) with the head overhanging the last by at least 3 m. The excavator is on the generator's side, bucket over the rim; the cabin on the other, its door facing out (or behind the hopper if that side is wall).
- **Checked.** `tests/screeningPlant.test.js`: no coplanar faces (both generator sides, three heap sizes), the head over its heap and clear of it, every trestle on the ground, colliders inside the model, 60 farms (water, roads, other heaps, pad level, climb), and a walking capsule up onto the pad and held at the hopper. One look of the seed 8 pit from eight sides.
- **Cost.** `smoke`: spawn 634 calls and 3.30M triangles, yard 2,717 and 3.86M, village 2,065 and 3.66M, wood 1,386 and 3.99M, hilltop 1,724 and 6.81M (budget 1,730): within the budgets, and level with #556's numbers.

## Start and finish cones say so (#559)

- **Why.** Jamie tried the pit climb (#472) and could not tell where it started. Every cone was orange whatever it marked.
- **Decided.** `cone(kind)` in `startLine.js` makes a start (green, START), a finish (blue, FINISH) or a plain orange cone. The lettering is on the white band, twice on opposite faces, turned to read along the way you ride; a lettered band is wider and sits lower, where the cone is wider. No big signs. Colour alone is not enough (green against orange), so the word carries it.
- **Where.** The lap line by the yard (start and finish in one, so green, 1.4 times size), the drag strip (green pair, blue pair, 1.4), the hill and pit climbs (start and finish pairs green and blue at 2.2 times, the gates between orange at 1.8). Amy's course stays orange.
- **Map.** `farmMapFeatures` returns `marks`: a green S square at each start and a blue F at each finish, so they can be found from across the farm. They are squares with a letter, not just a colour.
- **Prompt.** Riding up to a climb's start (within `nearStart`) and looking at nobody shows "Enter  Time the pit climb" (`startPrompt`, `yourAsks.js`); Enter opens the wheel as before.
- **Cost.** One shared material per kind and one lettered band texture each (512 by 96), so no new materials per cone.

## Open and shut the hut's door (#566)

- **Decided.** `E` at the hut's doorway (priority over the sit-with-her ask, which shares the space) says "Shut the door" or "Open the door", with the latch cue. Both leaves swing together on their hinges in `doorSeconds` (0.9 s, eased); it starts open, as built, and is not kept between games.
- **Blocking.** `hutBuild.js` returns a `doorCollider` across the doorway, disabled while open and enabled (`HutDoor.blocking`) from the moment it starts to shut until it has swung most of the way back. The girls do not use physics, so a shut door never stops them: they open it for a stay (`go`) and again on leaving (`end`).
- **The girls.** On leaving they flag `shutWhenEmpty`; once neither is in or within 2.2 m of the doorway the door shuts if `rain >= shutRain` (0.25) or `darkness >= shutDarkness` (0.6), else it stays open. The latch sound for her is `HutAudio.latch`.
- **Warmth.** A shut door lets none of the stove's heat out to the girl stood by it (`warmsAt`); inside is unchanged.
- **Pure.** `hutDoor.js` (`HutDoor`, `wantsShut`), tested in `tests/hutDoor.test.js`. Console: `farm.hutDoorOpen` is a flag; the Farm tab's stove job has an "Open or shut the hut door" button.

## Harrowing the field (#94, was TODO 200)

- **Settled with Jamie.** A grass field of its own in the grass field (the spray field is stubble in the south field, and molehills belong on grass), and chain harrows that the tractor or any quad with a rack can pull, hitched like the sprayer. It is a job on the chalkboard like spraying.
- **Rules** (`world/harrowing.js`, pure, `tests/harrowing.test.js`). The field, 24 by 16 m at x 26 to 50, z 8 to 24, is 1 m cells, each untouched or combed along z or along x. With the tines down, hitched and between 0.8 and 7.5 m/s the 3.4 m mat, dragging 2.2 m behind the axle, combs every cell under it; going back across a cell the other way changes its stripe and does not count again. 90% combed is done. It stays combed 5 farm days and is off the board for them; an older save has grown out, the molehills with it.
- **The field.** Chosen by scanning the grass field for a level rectangle clear of the track (more than 5.3 m), bogs, puddles, bales, the hay patch, the drag strip and the jump's run-up and landing line (a test checks each). Rise 0.7 m across it. The harrows stand at its west edge, not in the yard, which is full of trailers.
- **The carrier** is the sprayer's with a different model: `SprayerTrailer` takes a `kit` (model, measurements, pose, footprint) and `HarrowTrailer` passes the harrows'. `T` lowers the mat or lifts it clear and tipped up behind (`poseHarrow`). Tow load counts the mat as two things while it is on the ground.
- **The look.** The mat is one vertex-coloured mesh (steel and rusty links, tines, grass and straw tufts), about 5,000 triangles, hanging from a spreader bar on chained drops; chains are a rod and a few rings, not a mesh a link. The field is a skin with a texture of 8 texels a metre, stripes one texel wide in two tones; 16 molehills are one instanced mesh whose matrices go to nothing when their cell is combed. Looked at once in the running game from four sides and close up, in two rounds: the first showed chains as floating rings and a clumsy raised pose, both fixed.
- **Cost.** The first model was 59 meshes (the sprayer's kit of wheels, mudguards, lamps and textured galvanising) and CI's frame budget failed it: village 2,214 calls against 2,200 and hilltop 1,747 against 1,730, main being 2,181 and 1,713. Every mesh is a draw call and a shadow draw, and a trailer in an open field is in view from a long way off. Now `mergeColoured` folds the carrier into one mesh, the hangings, the mat and each wheel into one each (five), with no mudguards or lamps, and the mat casts no shadow. Measured on built copies of main and this branch with `tools/budget.mjs`, the branch is about 8 to 12 calls over main at the yard, wood and hilltop; main measured against itself differs by up to 75 calls at the yard and 36 at the village from run to run, so single numbers mean little.
- **Kept** as `harrow` in the farm save: `since`, `doneDay` and a digit a cell. The molehills are dealt from `farmSeed`, so a farm has the same ones each game.
- **The girls** say `harrowStart` and `harrowDone` (`npc/harrowLines.js`), nothing in the chooser and no notice. Lines are merged last, so only the last voice banks changed.
- **Debug.** Tune: "Harrowing", "Harrows carrier", "Harrows wear", "Harrows mud". Farm tab, Jobs, "Harrow the field": tines down and up, harrow half, finish, clear. Console: `farm.harrow`, `farm.harrowTines`, `farm.harrowed`, `job harrow done`.

## The road meets the lane with no gap (#588, from testing #388)

After #535 the road's markings were right but its tarmac stopped short of the lane. `roadStrip` began at the last sample outside the lane's west edge, and the road meets the lane at an angle, so that cross-section ran slantwise: clamping its vertices to the edge left a wedge of bare ground between the strip's end and the lane. The road is now built from its first sample (on the lane's middle), so every cross-section before the edge is squashed onto the edge line and the bellmouth is covered whole. The lane's tarmac is still under it, so nothing is drawn over the lane. Same cost: a handful of extra triangles.

## Snow on everything that faces up (#95, was TODO 201)

Snow was only in the ground shader, so a snowy farm was a white field with a green surround. Now the rest of the world whitens with the same weather `cover`.

- **One patch, not new materials** (`world/snowCoat.js`, `snowCoatView.js`): `coatMaterial` chains an `onBeforeCompile` onto every lit, opaque, static material, after any patch it has (wind sway, rust), and keeps its programme-cache key distinct. The fragment block turns the world-space normal into "faces up" (`holdEdge` 0.3, so walls and the undersides of branches stay bare), lays patchy white by a three-dimensional noise on the world position, and roughens it. Walls stay dark, which is what gives the hedges their dark sides.
- **Left out** (`coatable`): glass and water (transparent), lamps (emissive), the girls (skinned), the ground (`userData.snowOwn`; it has its own) and anything low under a roof (a floor, a bench, a bale: `indoorRoof` 2.4 m with `world.covered`), or the barn's floor would go white.
- **Tops go first.** The cover the shader sees is the ground's times `topsRate` 1.45, so roofs and hedges whiten sooner and keep it longer. The outer ring takes the same, which gives the white hills. The terrain shader's tarmac takes 0.4 more cover to whiten, so the road is grey-white first and clears first.
- **Melt shows** (`SnowLook`): while the ground's cover is falling, bare surfaces go dark and glossy in patches, more on steep and downward faces (the eaves). It builds over `wetEase` 45 s to a full melt of 0.0012 cover a second (a mild day loses 0.0025) and fades when the melt stops.
- **Vehicles** (`vehicleSnowAfter`): each has its own uniforms, so its snow is its own. It gathers while it snows (`carSettle` 90 s), never exceeds the tops, slides off at speed (clear in `carShedSeconds` 6 s above `carShedSpeed` 3 m/s) and thaws indoors (150 s). Not kept in the save.
- **Only a snowy game pays for it.** The coat is applied while a snowy game loads, or on the first frame snow or cover is set by hand, so a game without snow keeps its plain shaders. Things added mid-game (a bought vehicle, a donor) are not coated until the next load.
- **Not done:** evergreens heavier than other trees (nothing marks them), snow on the girls (#518), and a visibly different south face on melt (there is no sun direction in the patch).
- **Debug:** Tune "Snow coat". Console `weather.snowTops`, `weather.snowMelt`.

## The pit's climb wall: shallower, no chutes, and a gate of cones (found testing #578)

- Jamie rode the 20 m pit: the roads, fork, depth and floor room were right, but no quad could climb the steep wall. Measured up a chute's centre line the mean was 36 to 38 degrees (the first, 9 m pit's were about 30). Lengthening the chutes to 2 depths (a mean of 26) did not help either: a chute is a spur sticking out of the wall, 3 to 4 m wide with a drop to the floor on both sides, so there was never a lane a quad could hold.
- So the chutes are gone and the whole steep wall became the climb wall (`climbAt`, `climbRun`, `climbWidth`, renamed from `steep*`): the longest and gentlest of the pit's walls, a Gaussian across about 0.45 rad, `climbRun` 2.2 to 2.3 depths. Measured across it on four farms: 28 to 31 degrees in the middle band about 6 m wide, 25 to 27 on the upper half, the other walls about 40. `depth` is 13 to 16 m (was 18 to 22), because a smooth lane at 30 degrees up 20 m needs about 35 m of run and the floor never closes up past 0.78 of the rim (`runFor`). Jamie chose to give up some depth. All 60 test pits keep their screening plant (57 at 20 m with the first chutes, 51 with the longer ones).
- Three buttresses of hard rock stay, 0.3 to 0.5 rad either side of the line, so none stands in it. The pit climb's gates are 9 m either side (`gateHalf`), up the middle of the wall. The test checks the wall at five angles across it is 30 degrees or less on average, and that no buttress is inside the gates.
- The haul roads keep their sweep, so with less depth to lose they are a little gentler.
- The north boundary tree line (`boundaryTrees.js`) now skips the pit's field as it skipped the wood: it ran along z -88 to -101 and put a tree on the lead-in road. The pit's field has trees of its own.
- A climb's start and finish are one gate of two cones each (green START, blue FINISH), not a box of four, on the pit and on the hill pasture alike: eight big cones read as a muddle.
- The lip's fence is open where the climb goes over it, as it is where the road comes in (`fenceOpen`, the gate's width plus a metre and a half): Jamie had to drive through it at the finish.
- Heaps are kept clear of where the haul roads really finish (`descentPath`'s last point), not where `from` plus `sweep` guessed: with the shallower pit that guess was off, and on 32 of 60 farms a heap sat on a road's foot (Jamie found a small sand pile at one). Now at least 6 m clear on all 60, and every one still has its plant.
- The girls' line "Up the steep wall and over the lip" (`pitYou`) is a recorded line and still says steep; it needs re-recording if it is changed.

## A sheep field with terraces to ride up and down (#621)

- The two knolls in the sheep field are gone. The field's ground now steps down to the stream in broad terraces, like the lynchets on a real hill farm: two banks (`layout.hill.paddocks.east.terraces`: `banks` at z 102 and 118, each a smoothstep `width` 7 m across) and so three treads, one north by the wall, a middle one the gateway's ramp opens onto and a long one at the bottom.
- The steps take the place of the valley side's own smooth fall inside the field, not on top of it (`terraceLift` in `hill.js`): the bottom tread sits exactly at the height the pasture's north edge had, so the field meets the pasture with no step, and the total fall is only that fall (measured 1.8 to 1.9 m across the field), about 0.9 m a bank. Peak slope is 0.19 to 0.20 (11 degrees) before the 0.09 m rough, well under the 0.3 the farm's quads manage; a test holds it under 0.3 with the rough on.
- Every height outside the field is unchanged, tested point by point against the same layout without terraces over x -40 to 70, z 60 to 160: the banks ease in over 10 m past the lane gateway's ramp (so the ramp is exactly as it was) and out over the last 6 m before the east hedge. The old knolls had tails a metre past that hedge, so those few centimetres do go.
- `hill.terraceSlope(x, z)` is the banks' steepness (0 on a tread, in the field only). On the back of a quad moving over 1.5 m/s on a slope over `voiceTerraceSlope` (0.1), the girl says `terraceBank` (a new line each, Amy steady, Jess for taking it faster, in `terraceLines.js`, last in both sets so the earlier banks do not re-pack). The pasture's `hillSteep` is for 28 degrees of lean, so it never fired here.
- The girls drive the field as before: the route check allows a rise of a quarter of the run plus 0.3 m, which the banks pass. Not done: Jess taking the banks faster and Amy choosing a gentle end. The banks have no gentle end, being even across, and speed is the drive module's, so it was left alone.
- Console: `tp me to terraces` (the top tread) and `scene terraces`.

## The village road end, turned south (found testing #593)

- **The mouth.** #588's fix still left a wedge of bare ground on one side: only cross-sections lying wholly past the lane's edge squash flat onto it, and the road's first one is slanted. `roadLeadIn` (`village.js`) lays six samples 2 m apart out over the lane along the road's heading, and a strip clamped to the lane's edge starts from them, so the whole bellmouth is covered. Test: the widest cross-sections of every lead sample lie past the edge.
- **The road turns south.** Jamie asked for it to turn left before it leaves the village, with the closed sign on the south. The last points of `village.road` (layout.js) bend from the pub's end to run straight south to a gate 0.3 m before the last sample (`end.back`), 3.5 m short of the village's south wall. `S.gate` (position and heading) replaces `end.barrierX`: `roadEnd` builds the gate facing west and turns it to the heading, and lays its colliders across or along the road by which way it runs. The road past the gate (`end.beyond`) goes on south, rising 5 m, and bends east. Pavements, give-way line and the car park's mouth are unchanged, since the bend is west of the pub's entry.
- **Wall and hedge.** The south wall's drawn pieces stop at the hedge either side of the road past the gate, tucked a hand into it (`BEYOND_OUTER`), in even runs, and its collider stays whole. The hedge starts 0.3 m behind the gate's posts and its near end now has a face: it was open, so you could see up its hollow.
- **Cones.** The closed sign's cones are the game's `cone()` (`startLine.js`), each set on the road's surface at its own height: the group sat at the ground under the road's 7.5 cm lift, and the road slopes across.
- **Left for #536.** The pavements still stop short at the pub's junction, at the garage and at the first house.

## Placing a building's contents from the building (#661)

- **What.** `layout.js` now holds an `anchors` table (`barn`, `yard`, `hut`, `neighbour`, `village`, `pit`, `logYard`, `bonfire`, each `{ x, z, yaw }`), and everything that belongs to one is an offset from it inside `at(name, ...)`. `resolveLayout` (`layoutAnchors.js`, pure) turns it into today's absolute numbers once at load, so every reader of `LAYOUT` is unchanged and nothing moved on screen: a test compares every number with a snapshot of the old layout (`tests/fixtures/layout-before-anchors.json`) to a millimetre. Moving the farmstead (#682) is the barn's and yard's anchors, not about 40 numbers.
- **How an offset reads.** In the anchor's frame, turned the way Three.js turns an object about y. A `{ x, z }` is a point (its `yaw` turns with the anchor), `halfX`/`halfZ` with no `yaw` swap on a quarter turn, a `{ minX, maxX, minZ, maxZ }` stays a rectangle (quarter turns only) and a list of `[x, z, yaw]` is a list of points. A coordinate on its own cannot say which axis it is on after a quarter turn, so the resolver throws on one: such things were given their other coordinate (a door's `z`, the loft's `edge`, the ladder's `x`, the pumps' `spots`, a weather station's and the catalogue's `z` and `x`). `loft.edgeX`, `ladder.footX` and `pumps.xs`/`z` are read off those points once at the end of `layout.js` for the readers that want one number.
- **Barn, yard, village, neighbour, hut, pit, log yard and bonfire** hold what the issue listed; the bench, jumper rail, helmet hooks, weather station, charger and catalogue got an explicit `yaw: 0` so they turn with the barn.
- **Positions written elsewhere** are gone: `npc/spotNames.js` builds its 21 places from the layout (the anchors, features and `spawn.away`, with four open-ground ones in `layout.namedSpots`), and the console's `tp terraces` reads `terraces.stand`. The trough's spot is now the trough's own 146.8, not 147.
- **A turn is refused until a piece can be drawn turned.** The data turns, but the code that builds the barn and the rest still draws them axis-aligned, and a few scalars along an axis (`loft.strawDx`, the silage stack's corner) have no yaw. Turning the barn in the data alone left its spares rack standing in the field while the walls stayed put, and smoke still passed. So `resolveLayout` throws for an anchor with a non-zero `yaw` unless the anchor says `turnable: true`, naming the anchor and saying its builders don't honour a yaw yet. No anchor is `turnable` today. An issue that needs a piece turned first makes that piece's builders (and those scalars) honour the yaw, then sets `turnable: true`. The tests still turn every anchor by setting `turnable` themselves, so the data check stays; `tests/layoutAnchors.test.js` also shows the refusal and that a yaw of 0 needs nothing.
- **Checked.** `tests/layoutAnchors.test.js` also moves each anchor by (37, -12) and turns it a quarter, working out the expected numbers itself, and checks everything of that anchor moved or turned and nothing else did. No change to the frame budget: the numbers are the same.

## Fields are shapes, not boxes (#662)

- **What.** A field is a clockwise polygon with a kind (`FIELD_KINDS`: grass, arable, hay, ley, wood, pasture, rough, yard, lane), and a boundary is a line with a kind (`BOUNDARY_KINDS`: hedge, wall, fence, rail, none) and gaps. `src/world/polygons.js` is the pure geometry (point in polygon, distance to a side, a ray against a side, clamping in, the corners of a notch), `fieldPlan.js` builds today's farm as a plan, and `fieldMap.js` answers `areaOf`, `hedgeDistance`, `hedgeAhead`, `route`, `clampToArea` and `inFarm` from any plan. `createFields` is those, plus what is still the farm's own (tarmac, stubble, the wear bounds).
- **Gaps are metres along the hedge.** A gap or gateway's `at` is its distance from the hedge's first end, so a hedge can run any way. It was the x or z it sat at; everything that read it (props, the map, sight, the wildlife, the dog's walls) reads along-the-line instead, through `hedgedAt` and `pointAlong`. A gateway is two points `gatewayAcross` puts square to the side, one in each field, so a slanted side works the same.
- **Shared sides are one hedge.** The boundaries are a list of lines, not a copy per field, so two fields that meet have one hedge between them. A test checks every metre of every hedge is on a side of some field and no line is listed twice.
- **A notch.** Inside a field a straight line may leave it, so `route` also goes by the corners of a field that points inward (drawn in 0.6 m), and a field with none is as quick as before.
- **Today's farm is unchanged.** Every field is still a rectangle, built in `fieldPlan.js` from the layout's numbers, not copied into `layout.js` (`hill.js`, `woods.js` and `quarry.js` still own those numbers, and a second copy would drift). `tests/fieldsPolygons.test.js` checks a 2 m grid over the farm, the lane, the hill and the wood against `tests/fixtures/rectFields.json`, written from the rectangle version: the same field name at every point, hedge distance to a centimetre, `inFarm`, and the same `route` from a 20 m grid of places to five spots. `areas` stays as each field's bounding box for the stubble, the ground texture and the map's bounds.
- **Not done here.** Stubble and the ground texture still fill a field's box, which is right while they are boxes; the map draws the polygon. A hedge's colliders are boxes along it, turned for a slanted one (not exercised in the game yet).
- **A layout can supply its own fields (reopened).** With `layout.fields` present, `fieldPlan` builds the plan from data (`planFromData`) instead of today's code, and `createFields` needs none of `road`, `hill`, `woods`, `quarry`, `village` or `gate`. It is plain data, so `layoutNew.js` can write it: `fields` are `{ name, kind, points }`; `boundaries` are `{ points, kind, gaps: [{ at, half, ...extra }], ...extra }`, where `at` is metres along the whole line and a line with more than two points becomes one straight `boundary()` per stretch, each gap on the stretch its centre falls on; `gateways` are `{ a, b, boundary, at, half, gate, id, shut, outA, outB }`, the way from field `a` to `b` (or `'out'`) through the `boundary`-th boundary. With a `half` it is also a gap in that line (the one already there at `at`, or a new one); without, only the way across. A bad field name or boundary number throws.
- **The farm's outline.** `layout.outline` as a list of points is what `inFarm`, `outsideFarm` and `outsideAll` use; `createFieldMap(plan, farm)` takes the polygon or a rectangle. `fields.farm` is the polygon's bounding box, for `hud/farmMap.js`, `world/props.js` and `world/traffic.js`.
- **Walls, fences and rails are obstacles.** `hedgeDistance`, `hedgedAt` and `hedgeAhead` use every boundary whose kind isn't `none`; `fields.hedges` stays hedges only, as `props.js` grows hedges from it. Today's answers don't change, as every boundary of today's is a hedge.
- **Today's farm stays built in `fieldPlan.js`, by Jamie's agreement:** the numbers belong to `hill.js`, `woods.js` and `quarry.js`. `tests/helpers/planAsData.js` writes that plan out as data and `tests/fieldsPolygons.test.js` runs the golden fixture against both routes; `tests/layoutFields.test.js` builds a small farm from data alone (a slanting outline, three fields, a bent hedge, a wall, two gateways).

## A layout version in the save (#663)

- **What.** `LAYOUT_VERSION` in `layout.js` (1 today) is written into the farm save by `writeSave`. A save with none is layout 1. `openFarm` passes the save through `forLayout` (`farm/farmSave.js`), which, when the versions differ, returns it without its kept positions and says `layoutReset`. The first moment of play toasts "The farm has been laid out again. Your vehicles, money and the girls are as you left them." and saves, which writes the new version, so it is said once.
- **What goes.** Everything the save keeps as a place on the ground: the bales, the lane's ruts, the mown, cut, combed and sprayed ground (the hay stack's count and the sprayer's tank stay), what was lying about (`lostThings`) and which batteries were out and where (they go loose on the bench, as for any new day). Logs stay: that is a count, not a place.
- **What stays.** Everything else, including `validSave`, which still accepts every save version it did. A vehicle's own save has no world position, so none of it is touched.
- **Why at load, not in each restore.** One list in one place, tested against a fixture of a real layout 1 save with each position filled in (`tests/fixtures/farm-on-layout-1.json`, `tests/layoutVersion.test.js`). A new thing the save keeps as a position is added to `forLayout`'s list.
- **Not breaking by itself.** The PR that first raises `LAYOUT_VERSION` (#692) carries `💥 breaking`.

## One road network (#664)

- **What.** `layout.roads` holds every road: `id`, `surface` (`tarmac` or `stone`), `points` (a Catmull-Rom centreline), `halfWidth`, `pavement`, `markings` (`centre` dashed, solid or none; `giveWay`; `edge`), `verge`, `hedged`, `junction` (`{ end, onto }`), `level` (`shoulder`, `lead`, `blend`, `mouth`), `camber`, `pads` (round hardstanding), `passing` (spacing, length, bay), `cuttings` and `draw`. The lane, the farm road and the village street are the three today. The lane's and the farm road's numbers are written once as constants in `layout.js` and shared with `publicLane` and `road`, which stay because the barriers, hedges and gates read them; the street's points moved out of `village.road` (the fixture of the old layout follows).
- **One module answers.** `roads.js` is pure: `tarmacAt` / `surfaceAt` (a hand's width of soft edge, as before), `nearest`, `onRoad`, `levelAt(x, z, ground)` for the terrain, `pointsAlong` and `lanePath` for traffic and the map (left of the way you travel), `passingPlaces`, `cuttings`, `boundsOf` for the mud grids. `fields.tarmacAt`, `terrainHeight`, `roadMud`'s grids, the lane's traffic, `farmMap`, the girls' road pace and the road under you (`player.road`) all ask it. The village's car park and forecourt stay the village's own hard standing.
- **Junctions are end to side.** A road with a `junction` is cut square at that end, not rounded, so its pavement never spills over the road it joins. Levels agree at the join: the joining road's level eases to the parent's over `blend` m (8), and it fades in from the square end over `mouth` m (4) so the two never fight. Where roads overlap their levels are mixed by how far each reaches; a test walks across a junction at 1 cm and finds no step over 2 cm.
- **Cuttings.** A levelled road whose verge edge stands more than `MAX_CUT` (1.5 m) above its level is reported by `cuttings()`; it is fine only if the road lists the stretch in `cuttings`. Today's roads have none.
- **Levelling today.** Only the farm road is levelled by the network, exactly as before (level across, 3 m shoulder, starting 2 m before the tarmac, a flat hardstanding; `tests/roads.test.js` checks it against the old formula at 6000 points). The lane follows the ground and the village is laid to its own plane, which levels the buildings too, so neither is `level`ed here. Camber is supported (`camber` m of crown) and unused, as no road had one.
- **Surfaces.** `stone` is a surface of its own (`stoneAt`, `surfaceAt`), so the drive and tracks can use the same road data when they come; the grip, mud and map of a stone road are the next issue's to wire. No stone road is on today's map.
- **Drawing.** `roadMesh.js` is the one builder: a strip along the road's points (`roadStrip`), marks as decals (`paintMarks`, `dashes`), a kerb (`kerbFace`), and the tarmac texture (`tarmacPixels`: speckle, wheel tracks, a gritty edge and `patches` repairs; none on today's roads, so they look as they did). The lane goes through `buildRoadMesh`; the village's pavements, kerbs and bellmouth use the same strip and kerb functions with their own options. The farm road is `draw: 'ground'`: it is painted into the ground (`paintRoadOnGround`, same speckle, same random draws, tested against the old painter) because a mesh over it would hide the mud, rain, frost and snow the ground shader lays on it.
- **Checked.** The village bend is pixel-identical to main in a shot from the same spot; the lane and its mouth differ only in the tarmac's speckle phase. `tarmacAt` cost 0.09 µs a call (was 0.02) and 7 ms more once at load for the tarmac mask; draw calls are unchanged. That 0.09 µs was only for today's three short roads (see the next bullet).
- **An index so a long road costs no more per lookup (reopened).** The first version walked every segment of every road a lookup might be on, so a 3 km road (Back Lane, #686) made one `tarmacAt` 27 µs. Each road now has a uniform grid of 16 m cells (`GRID_CELL`), built once in `buildRoad`, each listing the segments that pass through it; the pads and passing bays are listed in the cells they reach (`indexAids`). `nearestOn` takes a `reach`: `coverOf` passes the road's hard edge, so a point away from the road finds empty cells and returns at once, and `nearest(x, z, within)`, `onRoad`, `onCarriageway` and a pad-less `levelAt` pass theirs. With no reach (`nearest` unlimited, a levelled road with pads) it widens ring by ring until no closer segment can exist, so it still finds the true nearest from anywhere. A road's two end segments are always looked at when there is a `lead`, as the lead runs them on past their cells. A tie goes to the lowest segment, as a walk in order did, so the answers don't change: `tests/roads.test.js` compares `tarmacAt`, `stoneAt`, `surfaceAt`, `nearest`, `onCarriageway`, `onRoad` and `levelAt` with a plain walk (`createRoads(defs, { cell: 1e6 })` is one cell, so every segment) at 20,000 random points on a winding 3 km road with passing places, pads and a junction, past each end and behind the square junction end and far off, and on today's three roads. A counted test (`counts.segments`) holds the average to under 30 segments a `tarmacAt` across the farm; it is 2.2 on the 3 km road, against 1,751 for a walk.
- **Measured in the real game** with a 3,006 m winding road added to a throwaway `layout.js` and a timer in `tarmacAt` (the 796,483 lookups of loading, then 120 drawn frames): all of loading's lookups 104 ms with the index (2,283 ms without; the timer's own cost is in both), 0.016 ms of lookups a frame (103 a frame), against targets of 300 ms and 0.3 ms. With today's roads alone: 95 ms and 0.013 ms.

## Wear in tiles (#666)

- `GroundWear` (unchanged, its tests too) is now the grid of one tile. `WearTiles` (`src/world/wearTiles.js`) holds a `GroundWear` for each 64 m square that has something worn or thawed in it, over all of `layout.ground` and the fine grid, and lets it go when it is clear. A tile's grid reaches two cells into its neighbours, so a wheel writes the points it lands on into every tile that holds them and a track across an edge reads the same either side (a test compares tiles with one big `GroundWear` at points either side of an edge).
- Fine (2 m, as before) in tiles that touch `fields.wearBounds`, 8 m in the rest, so a worn patch at the far corner of a 1.4 km ground is a wider, softer patch than one in the yard. Cells and the 64 m size are constants in `wearTiles.js`; nothing new is tunable. The lane's recovery, soak and wear rates are the existing "Ground wear" tunables, shared by every tile.
- The shader reads a table of one texel a tile (which atlas slot, or none) and an atlas with a slot of 36 by 36 texels for each tile that exists (the coarse tiles resampled to the same texels), so frost and snow thaw under tyres anywhere on the ground and the ground that has never been worn costs no texture. The atlas has one slot for each tile of the ground up to 24 by 24, so a 1,420 by 1,040 m ground has all of its 391 tiles; today's farm has 8 by 8 slots (0.33 MB).
- The save keeps the lane as before and in the same key, `lane`, now as tiles: `{ tiles: [[tx, tz, index, 0..255, ...]] }`, only the points that are worn, a byte each. An older save's flat pairs still load (`fromJSON` takes both). The general form (every worn tile) is there for later and in the test: four quads wandering a 1.4 km ground for an hour of play, wet half of it, leave 306 tiles and save as 9 KB (limit 200).
- Measured in node over 1,420 by 1,040 m (369,200 fine cells): the dense grid was 7.0 MB of arrays plus a 1.5 MB texture and 4.1 ms to repack each half second anything moved; tiles cost nothing until ridden over (the atlas, 2.0 MB, is the only fixed part), 1.3 MB after that hour, 0.18 ms a frame with all 306 tiles live, and an idle frame is 0.0001 ms.
- Today's farm, `tools/perf.mjs` before and after: draw calls and triangles the same at all five spots; ms a frame 6.7 to 6.6, 12.4 to 12.2, 11.5 to 11.2, 10.0 to 10.0, 11.0 to 11.1; "Ready" 3.28 to 3.26 s, 32.3 MB fetched either way.
- Not scaled with the ground: road mud (`roadMud.js`) has a grid for each road (#664), already sized by the road, and puddle ice is one value for each puddle. The painted texture is #665's.
- Seen: the green lane dry and set to `1` (`setLane`) from the south end, the ruts alone against a churned brown band with ragged edges. A first look showed no change at all: the table of tiles was written down the columns of a texture that is read along its rows, and the node test had the same mistake, so it now finds each tile as the shader does.

## The far landscape waits for the new map (#708)

- #708 moved from 0 · Foundations to 1 · The new map on 9 November 2026. Foundations is for what has nothing new to see, and the far landscape is a new thing to see that has to meet the new map: the ring starts at the map's edge and matches its ground, and its looks and budget spots are from the ridge and Back Lane. Built on today's 420 m farm it would be built twice.
- It depends on #681, #682, #686, #687, #688 and #713, and #692 depends on it, so the new farm doesn't open with its edges showing.
- The church tower's far version came out of it: #715 builds the tower and depends on #708, so holding #708 open for the tower could never finish.

## A piece not yet moved stays where it is (#681, the review of Foundations)

- Settled with Jamie on 9 November 2026. `layoutNew.js` starts from today's layout and replaces only what has moved, with `NOT_MOVED_YET` naming every place still at today's numbers. Each move takes its own off the list, and #692 needs it empty. The issues first said a piece not yet moved is left out of layout 2.
- Why: leaving out a piece stopped the game loading for 16 of the 21 pieces tried, with only the wood, the quarry, the neighbour, the muck heap and the log yard coming through, and each showed only its first error. Only 27 of the 510 reads of the layout guard against a missing piece. Guarding them all is a large job that nothing on the new map needs.
- A throwaway copy with #681's ground, outline, fields and hedges, a road of about 3 km and today's pieces where they stood loaded and played with no page errors, at about 1 ms more a frame. Old pieces sit oddly on the new map until they move, but only behind `?layout=2`.
- The same trial showed what #759 and #760 now own: "Ready" going from 3.29 s to 5.68 s and every road edge blurred if the painted square is stretched to fit (#759), and CI's load taking 79 to 134 s of the smoke test's 150 s (#760).

## A set piece that moves is measured from where it stands (#823)

- The rolling road trailer was hidden for the whole of the tuner's visit. Far detail draws a set piece within 160 m of its nearest edge and worked that edge out once, on its first look, since set pieces stood still. The trailer is built out of sight and towed in from the lane, so its edge was kept from the lane's end, about 300 m from the barn, and the trailer was judged by that spot when it was set up 21 m from you. It had been so since #505 hid it by distance.
- A set piece marked `userData.moves` now has its reach from its own middle worked out once (4.2 m for the trailer), and the box is laid round where it stands at each look, whichever way it is turned. That is a few sums a quarter second, so nothing is gathered again as it moves. The brushwood bundle is marked too, as it is carried and put down anywhere and vanished the same way over 160 m from where it lay. The rest are placed when the world is built and stay put.
- Checked in the running game with `road.call on` (a script in `.scratch/pp`): with the mark taken off, the kept box stayed at the lane's end all the way in and the trailer stayed hidden by the barn; with it, the box followed the trailer in and it was drawn behind the Land Rover once set up.

- Home Ground is only the play field: the Home Loop, jump, cones, line-up and bonfire. The hay patch, the harrow field and the log yard leave it for their own fields (#691, #683), the paddock fences go, and the boundary becomes post-and-rail with a gate from the yard (#696). The drag strip is not moved: #616 removes it after the move and before `?layout=2` becomes the default.

- Recommended, for #815 to place: one old oak on the rise with the bonfire ring beneath it as the field's landmark. Its sound (rooks, wind in the grass, a skylark in summer, the bonfire) was not listened to and is not in the code.
- Measured: the field's spawn spot is 596 draw calls, 2.47M triangles and 6.6 ms a frame, inside the 8 ms budget; the yard spot is 12.1 ms (#816). The heaviest things in view are the boundary trees (batches of 100k to 130k triangles), the stubble blades over the hedge and the yard's clutter.

The drag strip goes (#616). The jump becomes old equipment put to use, the cone course gets its order marked (#831), the track gets a lap timer of its own (#830), and the hedges, fences, gates, cattle grid, molehills, ruts, bonfire ring and a texture seam each have an issue (#828 to #837).

## The stubble fields, the farm road and the Dutch barn reviewed before the move (#670)

- The two stubble fields (0.9 ha each) become arable fields of several hectares in #681, and `layout.stubbleFields` and `buildStubble` are written for two named fields, so they go per field as data (#813). The south field's rise is kept; the north field's hollow is kept and gets standing water. The drive, its cattle grid and gates are #568, the Dutch barn, the cars' pad and the muck heap go with the farmstead (#682, #694), and the spray patch becomes a whole field (#691).

Single views counted in the dev server: the road from the pad looking west is 1,288 calls and 4.06M triangles, the north field 614 and 2.74M. The stubble clump is 24 triangles and is the heaviest thing in the fields (0.37M to 0.93M a view, 60,000 laid for 1.8 ha), then the hedgerow trees.

The drive's surface differs between the plan ("Stone") and #568's looks ("old tarmac"). I did not ride or walk it, so Jamie's walk is added when it is done, as for #669.

Three plain bugs that would have survived the move were fixed in #842: the barn's sheeting banded in thirds (a 3 m tile on a 4.5 m wall), the shed's two-tone wall (a damp patch baked into a 2 m stone tile) and its doorway's flickering (the lintel and the stone over it on one plane).

## The gravel pit reviewed before the move (#672)

- The pit moves into a field about 165 by 170 m (#685), 1.9 times today's 120 by 120 m, with its own gate onto the lane and a second from the stone track. Today's 7 m rim margin goes: the pit keeps its dealt size (88 by 69 m at the lip, 13 to 16 m deep; the climb is tuned to it), so the lane side has room for the lead-in, the bellmouth, a spoil bank and the container's hardstanding.
- Why: the review is to rebuild the pit once with its faults in. It has no landmark from a gate, no sound, and nothing that says disused except the plant: the excavator is bright yellow over rust, the cabin fresh cream, the drums new. The walls are saturated wavy bands that read as a painted cake. `quarry.js` assumes a gate in the south edge and a rectangle (`q.maxZ`, `pastGate`, `waitSpot`, `plantOk`), so the move gives it the gate's own position and a list of gates.
- The lane gate is shut, chained and solid (Jamie, 15 November 2026): a disused pit's gate. The haul road ends in it and the way in is the stone track's gate.
- Measured on this Mac's GPU: the pit spots run 8.1 ms at the gate, 8.3 at the lip, 10.0 on the floor and 9.8 at the plant (budget 8) at 1,069 to 1,372 draw calls and 2.6M to 3.1M triangles. The pit's own set piece is 147,000 triangles in 103 meshes, about 5% of a view; the weight is what it sees (the barley stubble over the lip, 565,000 to 829,000; the GLB trees; the ground). The four hedges of its field are 4,056 triangles in 8 draw calls, so #558's budget worry is small and the swap to post-and-wire is for the look.
- Folded into the move: #558. After it: #689, #696 and #690 (the second gate's waiting point). All 200 farm seeds tried were dealt a plant and 2 to 5 heaps, so the old note that the plant is not found a place on every farm's pit does not show in play.
- The grades are in `docs/detail-audit.md`; the findings and the sorting of the area's open issues are on #672, and the hand-over is in #685's "From the review". I did not ride or walk it.
- Jamie's walk (15 November 2026) added: most of the bottom of the pit is fine or already listed. Blocks: the stones have no texture, the tyres no inner texture, the pallet floats (its top boards sit 2 cm above its bearers), the gorse is just triangles, the dead trees are very basic and not well modelled. The pit climb's cones look strangely horizontal on the wall, and a flag in the ground may suit the climb better, for the pit only. There is no tree line between the stubble field and the pit, and too little at the top of the pit besides the container (recommended: a weighbridge and kiosk, a fuel tank and pump, stockpile bays, a tipper or dumper left standing, concrete pipes, fly-tipping at the gate to match its "No tipping" sign, a dead pole). The lip fence has no collision, so you ride through it; the hedges, gates, wall and pig pen's fence were checked and have it. All of it goes to #685.

## The wet meadow and pond reviewed before the move (#673)

- The meadow moves from 61 by 54 m (0.33 ha) to about 160 by 148 m (2.3 ha, seven times the area) with the pond still about in the middle (plan (112, 292)), so the pond keeps about today's size (22 by 16 m) and the extra goes into the field: rushes, a ditch, a second group of trees, poached ground and a long way in from the gate (#684).
- Why: the pond is a diagram of one (an exact ellipse, one depth, the stream's colour with no sky in it, an even ring of grass-like reeds, seven oaks in a ring) in a mown lawn, with no landmark from its gate, no sound but a wheel's splash, no stock and no gate. #815's list of field landmarks has no Wet Meadow entry; the review recommends one veteran oak on the pond's far bank with a long low limb over the water, which is also where #147's swing hangs.
- The green lane no longer passes the meadow on the plan and #681's gateway list has none for it. The review recommends one gateway from Home Ground at about plan (197, 296), level with the pond. `hill.js` builds the meadow as a rectangle off the lane (`west`, `gates[0]`, `pondCut` inside the hill's `profile()` and `weight()`), so the move gives it its polygon, gateway and dish.
- Measured on this Mac's GPU: the four meadow spots run 10.9 to 12.3 ms (budget 8; the yard is 12.2) at 2,089 to 2,727 draw calls and 3.4M to 4.2M triangles. The meadow's own things are about 450,000 triangles (tussocks 385,000, oaks 41,000, reeds 25,000, the pond 48); the weight is the tussocks in view (0.6M to 1.7M), the GLB trees and the terrain. At seven times the area the tussocks scale by area with a cap.
- Before the move: the Wet Meadow's landmark, #881. After it: #147, #710, #248, #563, #696, #690, #145 and #707.
- The grades are in `docs/detail-audit.md`; the findings and the sorting of the area's open issues are on #673, and the hand-over is in #684's "From the review". I did not ride or walk it.
- Jamie's walk (15 November 2026) added: the reeds and plants are bland (now a block), there are no trees across the meadow, the pond is too circular and sterile, there is no scenery, and the ground rises in a small cut on one side with the hill round it looking designed (the sunk lane's bank without its hedge, probably). He agreed the Wet Meadow gets a landmark: a veteran oak by the pond, #881, split from #815 because that list is at the 12-line limit. The move (#684) takes the rest, with trees and scenery scaled by area with a cap.

## The sheep field reviewed before the move (#674)

- The field moves from 59.4 by 54 m (0.32 ha) to about 225 by 125 m (2.8 ha, 8.8 times the area), walled all round with the stream on its south edge, the yard's pens gate at its north-east corner (#695), the field barn spot at about plan (378, 451) (#624) and a trough at about (300, 500) (#710). Recommended for the move: the banks run the long way along the contour, a bank every 30 to 40 m (three or four, not today's two), each still about 1 m and 11 degrees at steepest, the bottom tread meeting the stream with no step.
- Why: the review is to rebuild the field once with its faults in. The wall is 417 icosahedrons stacked in three even courses with grass showing between them and no taper or through-stones; the thorns are the hedge bush scaled up; the gateway is a bare mouth with a polka-dot mud patch; the south edge is open to the pasture and only `sheepFieldOk` keeps the flock in; the strip behind the wall is 12 m of dead ground; the grass is the hill's rough tussock where the plan wants the better grass; and the night is black. The field has no landmark but what #815 already lists, the barn, and no sound but the bleats.
- The green lane runs down the field's west side and #681's gateway list has none into it, only the yard's. The review recommends one in the west wall at about plan (195, 500), for a way up to the barn from the ford.
- Measured on this Mac's GPU: the four field spots run 10.0 to 12.3 ms (budget 8; the yard is 12.5) at 1,541 to 2,415 draw calls and 3.38M to 3.91M triangles. The field's own things are about 450,000 triangles, nearly all the tussocks (3,035 clumps of 144 = 437,000); the wall is 8,340 and the five sheep 4,180. At 8.8 times the area the same tussock density is about 26,700 clumps and 3.8M triangles: the in-bye wants almost none and a cap. The wall round is about 3,700 stones and 74,000 triangles near, with the far swap in place.
- Folded into the move: #622's wall round the field (the stile and the girls on it stay with #622 after it). Before it: #639's testing of today's banks. After it: #623, #624, #625, #695, #710, #696, #707, #691, #124.
- The grades are in `docs/detail-audit.md`; the findings and the sorting of the area's open issues are on #674, and the hand-over is in #684's "From the review". I did not ride or walk it.
- Jamie's walk (15 November 2026) added: the wall runs along one side only and joins nothing, so there is a gap at both ends (confirmed in the shots: the west end stops short of the lane's bank and the east end a metre or two short of the hedge), and it is not random enough to look real. The field has no other decorations or things of interest. The sheep do not walk (the legs are one static mesh moving with the body, so a sheep slides) and the model is basic, as the other animals' are: they want better models, perhaps open-source ones (CC0 only, credited in ASSETS.md), and the lane's ground beside the field is artificial again (the sunk lane's smooth, steep, hedgeless bank and ramp, as the wet meadow's). The move (#684) takes the wall as closed runs that end in the hedge or a squared end, the lane's ground and the scenery, with a flock's needs: a trough, shelter, winter feed and signs of stock. The sheep model is not part of the move: recommended as its own issue for all the animals, sheep first.

## The green lane, the ford and the stream reviewed before the move (#675)

- The lane goes from 54 m (z 80 to 134) to about 125 m (plan x 195 to 200, z 440 to 565, the border of Far Field and the Sheep Field), and the stream from 130 m to about 410 m, Back Lane's ford at plan (10, 572) to the river at (420, 572), with the green lane's ford at about (200, 575) and only 190 m from Back Lane's. The lane is cut about 1 m below the land and its bed falls with the new landform (#813); the stream gets bends, a pool and a riffle in place of one sine.
- Why: the lane is a grass trench (hedges came off in TODO 188's follow-up) with two ruts painted exactly 0.75 m either side of its middle all the way, the ford a stretch of stream with a hard-edged gravel box painted on the ground, and the stream a flat slate ribbon in the pond's colour down a bare trench. There is no landmark from the gate, #815's list has no line for it, and the only water sound is a wheel's splash. The review recommends an old ash leaning over the ford with a slab footbridge and a gauge post (Jamie's yes needed), a stream sound keyed on distance and `streamLevel.flow`, and one ford kit with a farm kind and a road kind for #686's Back Lane ford.
- `WearTiles` keeps 2 m cells only in tiles touching `fields.wearBounds`, so the move has to widen it over the new lane, ford and stream or two ruts 1.5 m apart go into 8 m cells and the lane stops wearing faster; the lane no longer stays straight north to south (see Jamie's walk below), so `fields.laneAt`'s strip follows the lane's line as well.
- Measured on this Mac's GPU: the four lane and stream spots run 11.4 to 12.6 ms (budget 8; the yard is 12.8) at 1,947 to 2,508 draw calls and 3.67M to 4.20M triangles. The lane and stream themselves cost almost nothing (ruts and bed are painted, the ribbon is about 270 triangles); the weight is the pasture's tussocks seen down it (1.8M to 2.2M, over half of everything in view), then bracken and gorse. At the plan's hill (13 times the pasture) the same tussock density is about 27M triangles if all were in view, so the count scales by area with a cap.
- Found on the way: the pale round dabs in the fields' mud (also in #673 and #674) are the 3,000-tuft scatter in `paintPaddocks` painted over the mud; skipping a tuft where `hill.mudAt` is over 0.1 removed them in the same shot. It goes to the move, which rewrites that function for polygons, not to its own PR.
- Folded into the move: #565's join at (420, 572) and #815's lane line. After it: #248, #563, #686, #689, #690, #696, #710, #833 (with the lane in its scope), #167 and #709.
- The grades are in `docs/detail-audit.md`; the findings and the sorting of the area's open issues are on #675, and the hand-over is in #684's "From the review". I did not ride or walk it.
- Jamie's walk (16 November 2026) added: (1) the grass is a little plain and untextured, probably everywhere: the ground's paint, not this area's, so the move does not try to fix it here (filed as #890); (2) the track is very straight and should wander a little; (3) the hills coming off the lane's sides still look like terraces, too artificial; (4) the track's colour steps from light to dark brown near the stream (the rut dabs choose their colour by `muddy > 0.2`, a hard switch in `groundTexture.js`); (5) the grass round the stream steps from lighter to darker with no blend (the silt strip's opacity only falls to 0.45 at its edge and the ford's gravel switches on at a fixed x); (6) the stream is not much deeper than the ford (0.32 m of water against 0.18 m, and 1.6 m half-width against 3.4 m). Two grades changed: the mud on the ford's approach is block, and the lane's line is a new block row. The move (#684) takes (2) to (6): a lane that wanders on a gentle curve inside its corridor (the plan changes first, its own PR, as it draws the lane at constant x), banks that come from #813's landform and not the hill's 30 m fade, rut and silt paint that blends over metres with no switch, and a stream with pools clearly deeper than the ford's 0.18 m (the ford staying shallow and crossable; the pools' depth is the move's to set, against the river's 0.45 m "deep" threshold for drowning wherever a pool can be reached).

## The hill pasture and the shepherd's hut reviewed before the move (#676)

- The pasture goes from 130 by 69 m (0.9 ha) to about 390 by 305 m (plan x 30 to 420, z 545 to 850), about 13 times, with the banks stretched along x as Jamie settled on 13 November. Their depth is not settled by that: today they rise 18.2 m over the first 53 to 60 m past the stream, but the plan's map draws six contours and the climb over about 175 m, and Jamie said on his walk that the banks stretch in depth too, so the hut is at the top of a long climb. The electric fence goes from 99 m along x at z 176 to about 300 m north to south at plan x 232, so it becomes a line between two points, with its gap where quads cross (the ford is west of it, the climb east), and the flock's `allowed` learns it (today the sheep walk through the wire).
- Why: today's hut and flask rock are 38 m apart and 55 m from the foot, so the girls' walk to either is about 42 s; on the plan each is about 250 m from the ford, where the girls wait, outside the 30 m and 120 m ranges within which a flask or a stay is offered (`flask.js`, `hutStay.js`; their 150 s and 220 s timeouts hold against those ranges), so a girl waiting there will not think of either: where girls wait is #690's. The hut is the best thing on the hill (graded good); the weakest are the gorse (giant flat triangles close up, a cylinder collider narrower and lower than the clump) and the wood's floor (a stair-stepped brown rectangle). The hill's wood (34 oaks, x -94 to -66) is in neither the plan nor #684: Jamie said keep it, made a thicker copse with a reason to go in (the hut's logs).
- The hill's landmark is the flask rock and the hut (#815), and neither reads from the gate or the ford: the hut is a speck by day and a black shape at dusk. The review recommends smoke and a lit window, a wind-bent hawthorn and a cairn by the rock (needs Jamie's yes). Its sound is only bleats, the fence's tick and the stove: one open-hill layer (wind, skylark, curlew, crow) is recommended.
- Measured on this Mac's GPU, four spots on the hill: 1,548 to 1,982 draw calls, 4.17M to 5.61M triangles, 10.2 to 11.5 ms (budget 8). Tussocks are 2.06M of 5.36M at the top and 1.52M of 2.41M at the foot; at the plan's area the same density is about 190,000 clumps and 27M triangles, and 260 gorse clumps with a collider each become about 3,400, so scale by area with a cap and collide per swathe.
- The grades are in `docs/detail-audit.md`; the findings and the sorting of the area's open issues are on #676, and the hand-over is in #684's "From the review".
- Jamie's walk (16 November 2026) added: gorse and stones are just triangles with no texture (the gorse model is shared with the pit's); nothing stands behind the top of the hill; the wood is too thin with no reason to go in; a hut that size would never have been taken up 38 degree banks, so it wants a gateway off Back Lane about 75 m away and a track with ruts; the electric fence's sound is very fake and you can walk through it (it gets a collider along its line, reversing the original "no collider"); and the hut's model has faults the move leaves alone (hollow untextured wheels, chocks 0.55 m from the wheels, an opaque window, a bland drawbar stone, a badly shaped coat, a bland shelf, no window in the door's top leaf), plus a door that did not swing, fixed in #895. #896 is the idea of a night in the hut, filed as a question.

## The hut's door leaves swing again (#676)

- Found on Jamie's walk of the hill (16 November 2026): pressing E at the hut's door clicked and blocked the doorway but nothing moved. `mergeByMaterial` in `hutBuild.js` merges every unnamed mesh into one per material, which took the boards, hinges and latch out of the two leaf groups and baked them in the open pose, so `HutHost.applyDoor` was rotating two empty groups.
- The leaf groups are now named `hutLeaf` and the merge skips anything under one (as it does the kettle). `tests/hutModel.test.js` builds the hut and checks the leaves hold their boards and that turning one moves its box; it failed before the fix. The host test never saw this because it mocks `parts`.
- Looked at once: the door shut from the front (`.scratch/a676/door.mjs`). The leaves cost a few more draw calls, no triangles.

## The river and its meadow reviewed before the move (#677)

- Why: the move (#565) puts the river through Brook Meadow, about 220 by 325 m (7.1 ha) against today's 46 by 85 m (0.4 ha), with about 230 m of river at full width against 44 m and about 235 m of brook to the village bridge. The river is `zAt(x)`, one function of x with ramps and a crossing all in x, so it can sway but not bend back, and it has no bends, bar or pool at all.
- It falls like a beck and runs the wrong way for the plan: the surface is 4.62 m at x 100 and about -2.2 m at x 35 (about 10% on average, 21% between x 90 and 100), where Jamie asked for a slow wide river (#444), and it flows west, where the plan's river runs east to the village (the land falls east: #813). The streaks even drift east, up the fall, from the code. The move reverses the flow, takes the spring and culvert ends off (the river rises on the Moor and the brook runs on under #688's bridge) and gives the fall a riffle at the crossing and a gentle slope (about 0.3%) through the meadow.
- Its landmark is the white-and-red posts and the willows, which read as nothing from the gate; its sound is none (nothing in `src/audio` reads the water but a wheel's splash). The review recommends the farm bridge (#689) with a crack willow beside it as the landmark (Jamie said yes on his walk) and a river layer made on the spot, a babble at the riffle and a murmur along the pools.
- The graded blocks are the water (a flat dark ribbon with no shore, bed or visible flow), the banks and bed (smooth planes of lawn), the willows (#564), the gateway (a bare mouth on a 12 m drop), the muddy landing (grip only), the brook past the meadow (a stripe with no banks), its spring end and the night. The river's own things are about 14,000 triangles of 2.6M to 4.2M in view: what costs is the tussocks (298,000 to 324,000), the GLB oaks (43,000 to 94,000 a tile) and the ground; four spots on the river measure 1,336 to 1,944 draw calls, 2.61M to 4.19M triangles and 10.6 to 11.8 ms (budget 8).
- The flashing in the water (#563) was not reproduced: a fixed camera on the water with the player moved dry, in the shallows and in the deep changed no more than the flow's own drift (0.13 to 0.26 of 255 a pixel), so the cause is something that moves with the camera (the ribbon's shadow, the wear layer under the water, the sun's glint); Jamie's walk knew of the flashing already and did not place it. Nothing in `src/npc` reads the river's depth, so a girl following you across walks the deep channel (from the code).
- Sorted: #813, #815, #684 and #689 and #563's run-out into the brook fold into the move; #564 (the willow model alone) and #563's flashing come before it; after it, the rest of #563, #248, #145 and #707, #696, #710, #709, #690, #688, #708, #890 and #444; #215 is the yard's culvert and goes with #710 and #817.
- A second opinion found that the water is in a 0.74 m pit at x 34, where the pasture's stream and the brook both cut the bed (`channelAt` stops only beyond the hedge and `cutAt` starts at it); the surface is about -2.2 m just either side. The move's new joint has a test that the bed is continuous across it.
- The grades are in `docs/detail-audit.md`; the findings and the sorting are on #677, and the hand-over is in #565's "From the review".
- Jamie's walk (16 November 2026) added: he knows of the willows and the flashing already (#564, #563) and did not place the flashing; the meadow is very bare (the move fills it, and #903 asks what else); a very small gap in the hedge between the pasture and the meadow (not placed; the hedge ring goes in the move, which tests every opening in a boundary); the stream's dirt paint stops square where the river starts, so the new joint carries it on; the reeds are very basic again; and the fence at the water's edge is very basic, where the farm has better fence textures. The hurdle's boxes date from 26 October and the shared fence model (`public/models/fence.glb`) from 4 October, with no reason written down for not using it, so the river gate and any river fence are built from it. The reeds and the hurdle become blocks. He said yes to the farm bridge with a crack willow as the landmark.

## The lane and its traffic reviewed before the move (#678)

- Why: the move (#687, with #568 for the drive and #686 for Back Lane) turns 410 m of straight lane (260 m of it open to you, shut in by seven invisible boxes) into about 920 m that meets the A-road in the north, bends and runs on south off the map, with no walls, passing places and a lane other people use.
- What survives: the traffic's pure logic (`Drive`, `Traffic`, `Visit`, `KidsGroup`, `GateMinder`, `RoadWet`, `RoadMud`) is sound and tested. What does not is where it gets its path and places: `roads.lanePath` returns only the road's two ends, so a pass is a straight line, `planVisit` assumes a straight lane (`laneX`, `gate.z - from * 16`), `Kids` runs from 12 m before the lane's `minZ` and `leaveRoute` drives to the lane's middle at z 200. The move feeds each from `pointsAlong` as waypoints.
- The lane's weather is missing because it is a mesh outside the ground's paint: dry in rain, white and line-less in snow, mud shed on it counted for grip and not drawn (#363). The move draws it through the ground near our drive and gives the rest a sheen and a gritted look.
- Landmark and sound: none of its own. Recommended a farm sign and a postbox at the drive mouth and the telegraph poles (#711) as the landmark, and a lane layer made on the spot (wind in the hedge, birds, a tractor and the herd far off) as the sound, besides real engines for the visitors (#122). Decision left to the move: with passing places the lane is single track and loses its centre line.
- Cost: the lane's own mesh is about 4,100 triangles and its traffic under 0.03 ms. The heaviest things in view are the GLB trees (215,000 to 664,000 triangles, in 19 to 76 calls), the stubble beside it (180,000 to 287,000) and the outer terrain ring (103,000 at every spot). Five spots on the lane measure 241 to 901 draw calls and 0.41M to 1.38M triangles without a fresh shadow pass; the game's own `perf` at the gate reads 8.5 ms, 711 calls and 2.02M triangles with shadows. The rabbits' one InstancedMesh (61,040 triangles in every view) is not the lane's and is worth its own performance issue.
- Found: the east edge of the lane has 1.3 m of detailed verge (ground to x 210, tarmac edge at 208.7), where a jagged green seam meets the outer ring; the move's ground ring removes it. No plain bug that survives the move, so no small PR.
- Sorted: #122, #547, #548 and #601 come after the move (the visit route and the kids' path are rebuilt by #568 and #687 first; #547 also waits for #867); #714 and #711 come after it; the rest folds in. The grades are in `docs/detail-audit.md`; the findings and the sorting are on #678, and the hand-over is in #687's "From the review".
- Jamie's walk (16 November 2026) added: one side of the lane is open and empty and looks unfinished, and the grass underneath does not map properly on the opposite side of the road (not placed; the 1.3 m verge and its hard seam on the east edge are the likely cause, so the move checks the new lane's east edge on the same grass); the road is "a little narrow perhaps" (4.6 m, `halfWidth` 2.3: a decision for the move with the single-track one, below); and the lane "stops very short beyond the village", which the move fixes. His walk was not on the review's first pass, and its Walk line was ticked on my shots before it, which was wrong: it is answered now.
- Not done: a kid in motion and a passing driver close up were not seen.

## The neighbour's farm reviewed before the move (#679)

- Why: the move (#569) takes a 57 by 56 m concrete yard on the lane's west side, with a house, a barn and three parked vehicles, to the plan's dairy farm across the lane (about 95 by 85 m, her herd and fields behind), so it was looked at once to rebuild it once.
- She has to be re-authored, not turned: the code assumes the lane is on the east (the gateway is two of the wall's five boxes in the east wall, `floorAt` eases 1 m on the east, the postbox and the gate's swing and the barn door face that way), the plan wants her gateway in her west wall, and an anchor can turn (`turnable`) but not mirror. `Neighbour`, her voice, `NeighbourHost`, the merge-by-material build and the far version's approach stay.
- What is missing against the plan: the dairy (parlour, collecting yard, bulk tank room, slurry store, north field gate), the herd and her fields, a landmark (recommended: the tank room by the gate with its stainless tank visible, a name board and a churn stand), a sound of her own (milking, stock, tank hum), a routine (she only stands by her door), light at night (only the windows glow) and a barn that is more than a shell.
- Cost: her yard's buildings, wall and props are 31 merged meshes, 29 draw calls and 2,800 triangles; her three parked vehicles are 382 calls and 133,000 to 136,000 triangles (93% of her calls) and her rig 20,000. The game's `perf` in her yard reads 8.6 ms, 713 calls and 2.02M triangles with shadows. The far version (82 triangles, swapped in beyond 45 m) is fine. The heaviest things in view from her yard are the GLB trees (280,000 to 1.3M triangles), the fields beyond and the outer terrain ring (103,000), as in #669 and #678. The move merges the dairy's boxes and rails like the house, puts the new buildings in the far version and instances the herd.
- Found: no plain bug that survives the move. A far-detail count taken right after a teleport reads the previous spot (the swap looks every quarter second from the camera), so `farDetail.update(camera.position, 0, true, true)` goes before a count.
- Sorted: #315 and #122 after the move (her vehicles' places and the visit route are rebuilt by it, #568 and #687 first), #714 and #145 after it; the rest folds in. The grades are in `docs/detail-audit.md`; the findings and the sorting are on #679, and the hand-over is in #569's "From the review".
- Not done: a ride at her gate (a scripted throttle moved no vehicle in the headless page), a girl walked into her yard, and her wave and turning in motion.
- Jamie's walk (16 November 2026) added, and where each went: a different house from the village's (#910); a different hairstyle and a proper outfit for her (#913); more in the yard (#911); the spots on the concrete too regular and plainly circles (#912); the dairy's things, which are #569's list; logs showing through the barn wall (#909, fixed) and she should leave her yard when she calls at the farm (#908, fixed), both plain bugs that my pass missed. Whether her vehicles make sense, whether she needs others and whether any should be driveable was asked of him in chat, with #315 as where borrowing would land.

## Her log pile no longer goes through the barn's wall (#909)

- Found on Jamie's walk of her farm (#679): the pile's middle stood 0.3 m off the wall, but logs run up to 1.25 m long across that line, so the ends of the longer ones (and their pale sawn discs) went 1 to 5 cm through the wall's 16 cm and showed inside the barn.
- `logPile(outer, random)` in `neighbourFarmProps.js` makes the logs (the same random calls in the same order as before, so nothing else in the yard moves) and stands the pile off the wall by its longest log's reach plus 2 cm. The collider uses the same middle and reach. A test over 200 seeds checks every log's near end is more than a centimetre clear of the barn's outer face; with the old 0.3 m it fails.

## The neighbour leaves her yard when she calls at the farm (#908)

- Found on Jamie's walk of her farm (#679): when a neighbour called at the barn, the woman in her yard stayed where she was, greeting whoever came near, and her Tor stayed parked, while a car in another paint and a driver in a green jumper turned up at ours.
- She is the visitor. `VISITOR_KINDS.neighbour` is her own car, the Tor parked in her yard (green `0x5d6d4a`, `K412 PBW`) in place of four paints and four registrations for neighbours who do not exist, so there are three fewer bodies to stock up. While `traffic.visit.kind` is `neighbour`, `NeighbourHost.syncAway` sets `Neighbour.away` (she does not watch, greet, chat, say goodbye or go indoors, and returns no events), hides her rig and takes her yard's Tor out with its collider (`setAway` in `neighbourFarmProps.js`). When the visit ends she is at her door again, facing the yard, and the Tor is back.
- One woman: `neighbourLook.js` holds her outfit and height, used by the rig in her yard and the driver who comes to the farm, so a change to either is one place (#913 changes the look).
- The route is not touched: the visit still starts at the lane's end and drives in along the lane, so she goes out of her yard when the visit starts rather than when she leaves her gate. The move (#569, #568, #687) and #122 rebuild the route, and starting it at her gateway belongs to them; this only keeps her yard honest.
- Checked: unit tests (`tests/neighbourHost.test.js` with a stand-in game and her yard built headless, `tests/neighbourFarm.test.js`, `tests/traffic.test.js`) and a headless run of `neighbour.go on` then `lane.neighbour on`: her rig and the third parked body (the Tor) are hidden during and back after (`.scratch/a679/away.mjs`).


## Nether Thornby reviewed before the move (#680)

- Why: the move (#688) takes a village 107 by 41 m, shut in by a drystone wall on the lane's west side, with a 106 m street that ends at a closed gate, to the plan's junction across the lane where the brook passes under a bridge, a street 420 m east to the A-road and the garage on the lane, so it was looked at once to rebuild it once.
- It has to be re-placed the other way round: the street leaves the lane's west edge going west and the plan has it leave the east edge going east, so the pub's car park and the shop come first, the give-way line moves from the north half to the south, the pavement gaps, the console's go-to places and the oaks, sign and phone box follow. Buildings face the street at `yaw` 0 or Math.PI, so no footprint needs turning, and each is placed from its anchor (#661). The garage leaves the street for the lane with its own bellmouth.
- What is missing against the plan: a street through (the wall, the closed gate and the road to nowhere go), about two dozen houses where there are five (all one plan, #543), front gardens (the south row's pavement runs to the doors), a landmark (recommended: the sign at the bridge and the church tower, #715), a sound (none but the chink at a counter), a night (the lamps light nothing, the canopy and the shop are black) and the garage's canopy and forecourt (#386).
- Cost: without its three parked cars the village is 300 draw calls and 63,000 triangles at the turning and 61 and 12,500 in the car park; the cars are 92 to 235 calls and 31,000 to 70,000 triangles each, 90% of its calls from the car park. The pub is 27 calls, the shop 35, the garage 27 to 32 with its forecourt 26, each cottage 19 to 24. The trees (302,000 triangles in 6 calls from the green) and the gorse beyond the wall (129,000 to 146,000) are heavier than the buildings. Its far version swaps in by 100 m up the lane.
- Found: no plain bug that survives the move. The street lamps' heads are joined now, so #539's first line is done; the bicycle at every second cottage is still in the code and was not seen.
- Sorted: #386 folds into the move; #392 waits for the move's new ground; #536, #537, #539, #542 and #543 are #819; #540, #541, #544, #361, #362, #393, #547 and #548 are after it. The grades are in `docs/detail-audit.md`; the findings and sorting are on #680 and the hand-over is in #688's "From the review".
- Not done: a ride through the village (a scripted throttle moved no vehicle in the headless page, as in #679), the MOT bay's inside, a girl sent to the pub or the shop, and parking a vehicle on the street or the forecourt for #392.

## The new farm's outline and fields (#681)

- **What.** `?layout=2` (the console's `layout 2`, or the button in "The farm that remembers") loads `layoutNew.js`: today's layout with the ground, the outline, the fields with their boundaries and gateways, and the stubble fields put in its place. `NOT_MOVED_YET` names the 79 places still at today's numbers by their key in the layout, each with the issue that moves it; a test fails on a key of today's layout that is in none of it, `SETTINGS` and `REPLACED`, and on a listed one that differs from today's. Layout 2 is never saved (`openFarm` treats it as a seeded game), so trying it never touches the farm you keep. `layoutNew.js` keeps its name until #692 folds it into `layout.js` (Jamie, 16 November 2026).
- **Plan metres.** Everything is written as the plan's map has it and moved by (-520, -365), so it can be read straight off `docs/farm-plan.svg`.
- **The clip.** `intersectPolygons` (`polygons.js`) clips any two simple polygons: the clip is cut into triangles unless it is convex (Far Field and Hill Pasture each have one notch), the subject is Sutherland-Hodgman clipped by each piece, and the pieces are joined by cancelling the sides they share. The outline is the subject, so its own notches cost nothing, and its east side lying along the fields' and the field lines running through its corners are handled.
